`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Three independent disable-slot paths exist today:** 1. `binary_sensor.py:_disable_slot_and_notify` — disables via switch service + persistent notification (used for sync failures and code rejection) 2. `zwave_js.py:_async_handle_duplicate_code` — disables via switch service + persistent notification (used for duplicate code detection) 3. `text.py:async_set_value` — disables via switch service only (used when clearing PIN on enabled slot) All three independently construct the switch entity ID using the same pattern: `{entry_id}|{slot_num}|{CONF_ENABLED}`. The shared helper will encapsulate this pattern and optionally create the notification. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Now, here's a design decision for you. The three callers have slightly different notification behaviors: - **binary_sensor.py** always creates a persistent notification with a custom title/message - **zwave_js.py** always creates a persistent notification with a different title/message format - **text.py** never creates a notification (silent auto-disable) I'm proposing this signature for the shared helper: ```python async def async_disable_slot( hass: HomeAssistant, ent_reg: er.EntityRegistry, config_entry_id: str, slot_num: int, *, notification_title: str | None = None, notification_message: str | None = None, notification_id: str | None = None, ) -> bool: ``` - Returns `True` if disabled, `False` if switch entity not found - When `notification_title` is provided, creates persistent notification - When omitted (like text.py's case), silent disable This keeps the helper focused on the mechanical "find switch + turn off + optionally notify" pattern while letting callers own the message content. Does that approach look right, or would you prefer something different (e.g., a `reason` string that the helper formats into a notification)?

Now PR 1b — extract `OneShotRetry`. This is a meaningful design decision, so let me outline the approach. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **The one-shot retry pattern** appears in two places with identical structure: 1. `binary_sensor.py`: `_retry_unsub` + `_retry_active` flag + `_schedule_retry()` + `_cancel_retry()` + `_handle_retry_callback()` 2. `zwave_js.py`: `_push_retry_cancel` + `_schedule_push_retry()` + `_handle_push_retry()` Both use `async_call_later` with a guard (idempotent schedule), cleanup on cancel, and a callback that clears the handle then calls the target. The key difference is that `binary_sensor.py` has an extra `_retry_active` flag to signal the callback is running (used by `async_update` to distinguish retry-driven updates from normal ones). `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Here's a design question for you. The binary sensor's retry has an `active` flag that the entity checks to allow updates even when the coordinator's last update failed. The zwave_js retry doesn't need this. I'm thinking the `OneShotRetry` class should look like: ```python class OneShotRetry: def __init__(self, hass, delay, target, name): ... @property def active(self) -> bool: ... # True while callback is executing def schedule(self) -> None: ... # idempotent def cancel(self) -> None: ... ``` The `active` property lets binary_sensor check `self._retry.active` instead of the old `self._retry_active` flag. Callers that don't need it just ignore it. Does that look right, or would you simplify it differently?

Let me think through PR 2a carefully before coding. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **The layer violation in `_get_slot_entity_states`:** The Z-Wave provider calls `hass.states.get(active_entity_id)` and `hass.states.get(pin_entity_id)` to read binary_sensor and text entity states. This is a provider (infrastructure layer) reaching into entity state (presentation layer). During startup, these entities don't exist yet, so the method returns `None` and masked PINs can't be resolved — causing the coordinator to report `None` for those slots, which the sync logic misinterprets. **The fix:** The coordinator already knows the *desired* state from config entry data (`CONF_PIN`, `CONF_ENABLED`). By storing `expected_codes` in the coordinator, the provider can read the expected PIN without touching entity state. The active binary sensor's state is a *derived* value (enabled + conditions met), but for masked PIN resolution, what we really need is "what PIN did the user configure?" — which is purely config entry data. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Before I start coding PR 2a, there's an important design question. The current `_get_slot_entity_states` is used for two things: 1. **Masked PIN resolution** (`_resolve_pin_if_masked`) — needs the configured PIN value 2. **"Slot expects PIN" check** (`_slot_expects_pin`) — needs to know if the slot is active (enabled + conditions met) For #1, the config entry `CONF_PIN` data is sufficient — we don't need entity state. For #2, the `ATTR_ACTIVE` binary sensor state reflects enabled + conditions. But the config entry has `CONF_ENABLED` (whether the slot is enabled) and `CONF_PIN` (whether a PIN is set). The condition entity state is harder to get without reading the binary sensor. However, looking at how `_slot_expects_pin` is actually used — it's only for ignoring stale `userIdStatus=AVAILABLE` events. The check is: "active=ON and PIN is set." `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **PR 2b design:** The goal is to move retry from ZWaveJSLock to BaseLock so any future push provider gets it for free. The approach: 1. BaseLock gets a `_push_retry: OneShotRetry` that wraps the subclass `subscribe_push_updates` call 2. The current `subscribe_push_updates` in BaseLock becomes `_subscribe_push_updates` (internal, subclass overrides this) 3. A new `@final subscribe_push_updates` in BaseLock: calls the subclass method, catches exceptions, schedules retry on failure 4. ZWaveJSLock's `_ensure_push_retry`, `_push_retry` field, and retry scheduling are removed Wait — there's a subtlety. ZWaveJSLock's `subscribe_push_updates` handles two types of failure: - `_get_client_state()` not ready → schedule retry (before even trying to subscribe) - `node.on()` raises `ValueError` → schedule retry (subscription failed) Both are provider-specific failure modes. BaseLock can't know about `_get_client_state()`. The clean approach is: subclass's `subscribe_push_updates` raises on failure, and BaseLock catches + retries. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Here's the design for PR 2b: - Rename BaseLock's `subscribe_push_updates` to `_subscribe_push_updates_impl` (subclass overrides) - New `@final subscribe_push_updates` on BaseLock: calls impl, catches exceptions, schedules `OneShotRetry` - ZWaveJSLock overrides `_subscribe_push_updates_impl` — raises when not ready instead of silently scheduling retry - ZWaveJSLock removes `_push_retry`, `_ensure_push_retry` - BaseLock owns `_push_retry` and `unsubscribe_push_updates` cancels it One question: the current ZWaveJSLock `subscribe_push_updates` does an early-return check (`_get_client_state` not ready) before even trying. With the new approach, this would raise an exception that BaseLock catches. But `subscribe_push_updates` is `@callback` (sync), so it can't be caught with try/except on an async call. This means BaseLock's wrapper also needs to be sync. Let me implement it:

Removes business logic from `text.py`. **Key files:** - New `sync.py` — the `SlotSyncManager` class - `binary_sensor.py` — stripped down to observer - `__init__.py` — creates managers during setup - `text.py` — remove auto-disable logic (PR 3b) This is the highest-effort phase and may not be worth it if the binary sensor is working reliably as-is. The value is architectural clarity, not bug fixes.

> AGENT

No race condition risk. Here's why: The current design already handles this with `asyncio.Lock` (line 278, used at line 595). Multiple triggers (state change events, coordinator updates, poll, retry callback) can all call `_async_update_state`, but they serialize through the lock. Each caller reads the *latest* state when it runs, so stale calls are harmless — they see up-to-date data and exit early if already in sync. In the new design, the entity still owns the `asyncio.Lock` and calls `manager.async_check_and_sync(slot_state)` inside it. The manager itself is stateless with respect to input — it receives a pre-resolved `SlotState` snapshot. The only mutable state on the manager is `_in_sync`, `_sync_attempt_count/first`, and the `OneShotRetry`. All of these are accessed single-threaded (HA event loop) and serialized by the entity's `asyncio.Lock`. The `OneShotRetry` callback goes through `entity.async_update` → `entity._async_update_state` → same lock. So the retry path also serializes. No new race conditions. The serialization model is identical to today — it just crosses the entity→manager boundary inside the lock.

> AGENT

You're right — I was thinking about it from a single entity's perspective but missed the cross-entity case. The scenario: 3 in-sync entities (one per lock × slot) each have their own `asyncio.Lock`. A config entry update (e.g., user changes PIN) triggers state changes on the active, PIN, and name entities. All 3 in-sync entities see these state changes and each tries to sync independently. Since each has its own lock, they can all run concurrently. But that's actually fine today — each entity syncs its own lock independently. Lock 1's sync doesn't interact with Lock 2's sync. The `asyncio.Lock` serializes operations *per lock × slot*, not globally. The real issue you're raising is different: **within a single entity**, the `_resolve_slot_state` reads active, PIN, and name entity states as separate `hass.states.get()` calls. If these states are updating rapidly (e.g., PIN text entity state updates, then active binary sensor state updates a moment later), the entity could read a partially-updated snapshot — new PIN but old active state, or vice versa. This is already a problem today in the binary sensor. The `asyncio.Lock` serializes sync *operations* but doesn't make the state reads atomic. With the sync manager, the boundary is the same: the entity reads states and builds a `SlotState`, then passes it atomically to the manager. The atomicity concern is in the entity's `_resolve_slot_state`, not the manager. Options: 1. **Accept it** (current behavior) — HA is single-threaded, so within one callback invocation, states don't change mid-read. The risk is across separate event loop ticks. 2. **Snapshot coordinator + entity states together** — read all states in one `@callback` (sync, single event loop tick), guaranteeing no interleaving. Currently `_resolve_slot_state` is called from both sync callbacks and async paths. When called from a `@callback` context, reads are already atomic within that tick. …

> AGENT

No real concerns with option 2. The state reads are already sync (no `await` between them), so in practice they're already atomic on HA's single-threaded event loop. Making this explicit by ensuring `_resolve_slot_state` is always called from a `@callback` context just documents what's already true. The "leakage" question — the entity already resolves states into `SlotState` before passing to the manager. That boundary doesn't change. The entity is the right place to read entity states; the manager never touches `hass.states`. So there's no new leakage. The only complexity would be if we tried to make the manager do its own atomic snapshot — that *would* be leakage. But we're not doing that. The entity builds the snapshot, the manager consumes it. Clean boundary. I'll add a note to the plan that `_resolve_slot_state` must remain `@callback`-safe (no awaits between state reads), then exit plan mode.

The `_in_sync` state is left as `False` (set on line 404 before `_perform_sync` is called) with no retry scheduled 3. The user sees "out of sync" permanently with no explanation and no recovery path 4. The exception surfaces as an unhandled task exception in Home Assistant's log, disconnected from any lock_code_manager context **Hidden Errors:** `ProviderNotImplementedError`, `HomeAssistantError`, `asyncio.TimeoutError`, `asyncio.CancelledError`, `OSError`, `TypeError` (if provider returns wrong type), `AttributeError` (if provider state is corrupted). **User Impact:** The slot shows permanently out-of-sync with no way to recover other than restarting Home Assistant. <task-notification> <task-id>ad5d375ccc98a914e</task-id> <tool-use-id>REDACTED</tool-use-id> <output-file>/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-raman-projects-lock-code-manager/90a13193-20ad-4705-9d38-7f3904ccf18c/tasks/ad5d375ccc98a914e.output</output-file> <status>completed</status> <summary>Agent "Code review PR 946 - second pass" completed</summary> <result>Both occurrences use `retry.pending`. Now let me confirm this was part of the fix (item 4 in the review task says "was `retry.active` before"). The task says the fix changed from `retry.active` to `retry.pending`. Let me think about which is correct: - `retry.active` = True while the callback is **executing** - `retry.pending` = True while a retry is **scheduled but not yet executing** The guard `not self._coordinator.last_update_success and not self._retry.pending` means: "if coordinator is unhealthy and NO retry is pending, skip." The intent seems to be: allow proceeding if a retry is already scheduled (because we're in a retry loop trying to recover). But using `pending` only covers the "scheduled but waiting" phase, not the "currently executing" phase. When the retry fires (`_fire`), the sequence is: 1. `_unsub = None` → `pending = False` 2. `_active = True` 3. Calls `_async_poll_sync()` 4. In `_async_poll_sync`, the guard `not self._coordinator.last_update_success and not self._retry.pending` evaluates to `True and True = True`, causing return — **the retry does nothing if coordinator is still unhealthy**. This means if the coordinator refresh fails AND we're in `_perform_sync`'s retry path, when the retry fires and coordinator is still unhealthy, the retry attempt is silently skipped. The retry won't reschedule itself (since `_async_poll_sync` exits early), so the recovery loop stops. This is a genuine bug introduced by the fix. Using `retry.active` (the original) would have been correct for covering the "currently executing" case, but would miss the "scheduled but waiting" case. The correct fix should be `self._retry.pending or self._retry.active`. --- Reviewing the current state of `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/sync.py` and `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/binary_sensor.py` after commit 376e67e. ## Second-Pass Review Summary All 7 checklist items were verified. When the retry fires, `OneShotRetry._fire()` clears `_unsub` (making `pending` False) before setting `_active` True. So inside the retry callback, `pending` is False and `active` is True — the guard `not self._retry.pending` evaluates to True, causing the retry to skip itself. The fix: use `self._retry.pending or self._retry.active` in both guards. Let me fix this now while waiting for the second review:

Therefore `except Exception` at line 314 will **not** catch `CancelledError` -- it will propagate correctly, which is the desired behavior for task cancellation. The handler itself is well-structured: `_LOGGER.exception(...)` logs the full traceback at ERROR level (appropriate for truly unexpected errors), and `self._retry.schedule()` ensures the slot does not get permanently stuck out-of-sync. > AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **HA's pattern:** - `helpers/` — things that help entities/platforms work (registries, debounce, dispatcher, config validation, event tracking) - `util/` — pure utilities with no HA dependencies (async helpers, dt, color, json, enum) Mapping to our modules: - `helpers/` feel: `callbacks.py` (entity callback registry), `data.py` (config entry data helpers), `helpers.py` (lock instance creation) - `util/` feel: `util.py` (OneShotRetry, mask_pin, async_disable_slot), `sync.py` (SlotSyncManager — though this is more than a utility) `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Thinking about this more — `sync.py` is a domain-level manager, not really a "helper" or "util". And we only have 5 modules to move. Splitting into two subpackages (`helpers/` + `util/`) for 2-3 files each feels over-organized. What about a single `helpers/` package (matching HA's convention) for all the internal modules? ``` custom_components/lock_code_manager/ __init__.py binary_sensor.py, sensor.py, text.py, ... # HA platforms const.py, coordinator.py, entity.py, exceptions.py # top-level helpers/ __init__.py callbacks.py # entity callback registry data.py # config entry data helpers lock.py # lock instance creation (renamed from helpers.py) sync.py # SlotSyncManager util.py # OneShotRetry, mask_pin, async_disable_slot providers/ ... ``` The only awkward one is `helpers.py` → `helpers/lock.py` (renaming to avoid `helpers/helpers.py`). Or we could merge its two functions into `__init__.py` of the helpers package. How does this feel?

look at data, helpers, and util. They can probably be combined somehow or redistributred

> AGENT

Here's what I see: **`data.py`** (47 lines): - `LockCodeManagerConfigEntryData` dataclass — runtime data type - `LockCodeManagerConfigEntry` type alias - `get_entry_data()` / `get_slot_data()` — config entry accessors **`helpers.py`** (101 lines): - `async_create_lock_instance()` — creates BaseLock from registry entry - `get_locks_from_targets()` — resolves area/device/entity targets to locks **`util.py`** (153 lines): - `mask_pin()` — pure utility, no HA state - `async_disable_slot()` — slot operation helper - `OneShotRetry` — retry scheduling class Natural groupings: - `data.py` contents are really **types + config accessors**. The `LockCodeManagerConfigEntryData` dataclass and type alias are used everywhere as type annotations. `get_entry_data`/`get_slot_data` are config entry plumbing. These belong alongside `const.py` and `exceptions.py` at the top level — they're foundational types. - `helpers.py` functions are **lock resolution** — they find/create lock instances from targets. Only used by `__init__.py` and `websocket.py`. This is integration setup plumbing. - `util.py` has three unrelated things: `mask_pin` (pure function), `async_disable_slot` (slot operation), `OneShotRetry` (scheduling primitive). These serve different consumers. Honestly, given the sizes — `data.py` is 47 lines, `helpers.py` is 101 lines — merging them doesn't save much and loses clarity. The real question is: what goes into `helpers/` as a package? I'd propose: - **`data.py` stays top-level** (it's a foundational type used everywhere, like `const.py`) - **`helpers/`** package contains: `callbacks.py`, `lock.py` (renamed from `helpers.py`), `sync.py`, `util.py` That gives 4 internal modules in `helpers/` and keeps the top-level clean with just HA-standard + foundational files. Does that direction work, or do you want to merge some of these more aggressively?

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` This audit surfaced some genuinely important architectural issues that go beyond code style. The most interesting ones are about **hidden state machines** and **temporal coupling** — patterns where correctness depends on things happening in a specific order that isn't enforced by the code. These are the kind of issues that cause hard-to-reproduce bugs months later when someone makes an innocent-looking change. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Excellent audit. Let me distill the findings into what's actionable, ranked by how hard they make the system to reason about: ## Top Architectural Issues ### 1. The data/options temporal window (LATENT BUG) `_update_config_entry` writes to `config_entry.data` directly. During initial setup, data is empty (config lives in options). If any entity triggers `_update_config_entry` during that window → `KeyError`. This is a real bug waiting to happen. ### 2. `CONF_LOCKS` means two different things - `hass.data[DOMAIN][CONF_LOCKS]` → `dict[str, BaseLock]` (global registry) - `config_entry.data[CONF_LOCKS]` → `list[str]` (entity IDs) Same constant, different types, different scopes. The unload guard uses `""` as default instead of `[]`, which works by accident. ### 3. Five paths trigger coordinator refresh For Z-Wave (push mode), paths 3-6 can all fire concurrently. Path 3 (sync manager) calls `async_refresh()` directly instead of `async_request_refresh()`, bypassing HA's deduplication. ### 4. `_in_sync = None` can stick forever If `_handle_coordinator_update` fires while `_aio_lock` is held during initial load, the update is dropped. No retry. Entity stays uninitialized until next state change or poll. ### 5. Lock removal cascade is unordered Entity removal tasks (async) and coordinator shutdown (sync) can race. SlotSyncManager could call a shutting-down coordinator. ### 6. Implicit state machines (sync state, connection state) `_in_sync` (None/True/False) and `_last_connection_up` (None/True/False) are tri-state machines with no formal model. --- Before I propose solutions, I want your take on priorities. Your "ticking system" idea is interesting — it could address #3 …

Now I have solid context from the memory files. Let me create a comprehensive summary for you based on what I've gathered: ## Summary The lock_code_manager project has significant architectural complexity centered around three main areas: **resilience/sync orchestration scattered across multiple layers**, **dual storage pattern ambiguity**, and **state tracking challenges** that make the codebase hard to reason about. ### Core Architectural Issues **1. Scattered Resilience Logic (Binary Sensor as Sync Orchestrator)** The binary sensor entity has become a disguised sync orchestrator rather than a simple state representation. Sync orchestration, retry scheduling, circuit-breaking, and slot disabling are fragmented across multiple modules: - `binary_sensor.py` - Manages active status and in-sync state - `zwave_js.py` - Provider-specific sync logic - `text.py` - PIN configuration with sync side effects - `_base.py` - Rate limiting and base provider logic This violates layer separation: provider code reaches into entity state, and two uncoordinated paths can disable the same slot. The recent commits (c13ca64, f53f077, 2220c36) show ongoing efforts to decompose complex closures and extract reusable helpers (`find_entry_for_lock_slot`, `build_slot_unique_id`), indicating recognition of this problem. **2. Dual Storage Pattern Complexity** Config entries use both `data` and `options` fields simultaneously (per AGENTS.md lines 95-96). Let me think through how. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **The core problem:** The current system is event-driven with multiple independent triggers (state changes, coordinator updates, retries, polls, connection recovery) all converging on the same sync logic. Each trigger has its own guards, its own timing assumptions, and its own edge cases. The "discard if locked" pattern exists because these triggers can pile up faster than sync can process them. **A ticking system** inverts this: instead of "react immediately to every event," you "mark dirty, then periodically reconcile." Events set a flag, and a periodic tick reads the latest state and acts. This eliminates: - #3 (refresh proliferation): One tick, one refresh cycle - #4 (`_in_sync` stuck): The tick always runs regardless of lock state - #6 (5 refresh paths): All paths just mark dirty, the tick does the actual work `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` The idea is: events stop triggering sync directly. Instead they mark the slot as "needs check." A periodic reconciliation loop (the "tick") picks it up and does the actual work. ``` Current (event-driven): state change ──→ _async_check_and_sync() coordinator ──→ _async_check_and_sync() retry timer ──→ _async_poll_sync() ──→ _async_check_and_sync() HA poll ──→ _async_poll_sync() ──→ _async_check_and_sync() Proposed (tick-based): state change ──→ mark dirty coordinator ──→ mark dirty retry timer ──→ (removed, tick handles it) HA poll ──→ (removed, tick handles it) tick (every N seconds) ──→ if dirty: read latest state, sync ``` **What this simplifies:** - No `asyncio.Lock` needed — the tick is the only writer, and it's sequential - No "discard if locked" — events just set a flag - No `OneShotRetry` for sync — the tick retries naturally on the next cycle - No 5 refresh paths — the tick handles all of them - `_in_sync = None` can't …

The `LockDisconnected` case is correct — it is a transient error and retry makes sense. However, the bare `except Exception` branch also sets `_dirty = True`. This catch-all is meant for unexpected errors, but if the error is actually permanent (e.g., a provider bug that raises the same exception on every call), the tick loop will retry forever at 5-second intervals: `_dirty=True` → tick fires → `_perform_sync` → exception → `_dirty=True` → tick fires → repeat. More importantly, there is a subtle gap for `CodeRejectedError`. That path calls `_disable_slot` and returns `False` without setting `_dirty`. But `calculate_in_sync` will still return `False` after `_disable_slot` is called asynchronously (the switch entity goes off on the next HA event loop iteration, not synchronously), so on the **next tick** the slot will still appear out of sync and `_perform_sync` will be called again — until `_disable_slot` actually takes effect. The circuit breaker `_sync_attempts_exceeded` is only checked before calling `_perform_sync`, so a `CodeRejectedError` will re-run the operation on every tick between when `_disable_slot` is called and when the enabled switch entity flips to `off`. Given the slot disable is an async task, there is at least one more tick window where the slot looks out-of-sync, and `_perform_sync` is invoked again, potentially firing the …

Here is my analysis. --- ## Error Handling and State Safety Audit -- PR #953 ### ISSUE 1: Race between `_dirty = False` and concurrent `_mark_dirty` during `await` **Location:** `/custom_components/lock_code_manager/sync.py`, `_async_tick` method (lines around the `self._dirty = False` assignment and subsequent `await` calls) **Severity:** CRITICAL **Issue Description:** `_async_tick` sets `self._dirty = False` at the top, then executes multiple `await` points: `_perform_sync` (which calls `async_internal_set_usercode` or `async_internal_clear_usercode`) and `coordinator.async_refresh()`. At every one of those `await` points, the event loop can run other callbacks. If a state change fires `_mark_dirty` (setting `_dirty = True`) during any of those awaits, the dirty flag IS preserved -- the flag was already cleared at the top, so a new `True` set during the await survives. This is actually **correct by design**. However, there is a subtle gap: if `_mark_dirty` fires between the `self._dirty = False` line and the `self._resolve_slot_state()` call, the new state change is acknowledged (dirty was set to True) but the slot state read by `_resolve_slot_state` already reflects that new change (since state reads are synchronous). So the tick processes the new state, then on exit the dirty flag is still True, causing an unnecessary extra tick. This is harmless -- at worst it causes one redundant tick. **Verdict:** The dirty flag pattern is safe in Home Assistant's single-threaded event loop. State changes during `await` points correctly set dirty, and the next tick will re-read. **No bug here.** This concern is unfounded. --- ### ISSUE 2: `_async_tick` initial load returns without setting `_dirty = True` on invalid active state **Location:** `/custom_components/lock_code_manager/sync.py`, `_async_tick`, the `if self._in_sync is None` block: ```python if slot_state.active_state not in (STATE_ON, STATE_OFF): _LOGGER.debug( "%s: Active entity has invalid state '%s'", self._log_prefix, slot_state.active_state, …

we're confident this is a better design? are there other designs we should consider?

Good question to pause on before merging. Let me think about alternatives honestly. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **The tick model trades responsiveness for simplicity.** The question is whether that trade-off is the right one for *this* system — a lock code manager where correctness matters more than speed. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` ## Three designs compared ### 1. Current PR: Pure tick (dirty flag + 5s interval) **Strengths:** One code path for sync. No concurrency primitives. Natural retry. Trivial to reason about. **Weaknesses:** 5s latency. Periodic wake-up even when nothing changes (though the dirty flag minimizes this). State tracking subscriptions exist *only* to set a flag — feels like overhead for a simple result. ### 2. Previous: Event-driven (asyncio.Lock + OneShotRetry + discard-if-locked) **Strengths:** Near-instant response. No wasted work. **Weaknesses:** 5 trigger paths. Concurrency complexity. Multiple bugs found across 4 review rounds. Hard to reason about correctness. ### 3. Hybrid: Event-kick with debounce Events don't just set dirty — they schedule an immediate tick via HA's `Debouncer`: ```python self._debouncer = Debouncer(hass, _LOGGER, cooldown=2.0, immediate=True, function=self._async_reconcile) # Event handler: await self._debouncer.async_call() ``` **Strengths:** Near-instant response (fires immediately on first event, then debounces for 2s). No periodic wake-up. One code path (the debouncer's function). HA provides the Debouncer — battle-tested, used by DataUpdateCoordinator itself. **Weaknesses:** Slightly more complex than pure tick. No periodic fallback (if dirty flag is lost, nothing retries). Would need a separate fallback timer for stuck states. ## My honest assessment The pure tick (#1) is the **simplest to reason about** and the **safest**. It eliminated real bugs that existed in the event-driven model. The 5s latency is acceptable per your earlier decision. The hybrid debouncer (#3) is worth considering if the 5s latency bothers you later — it gives instant response with HA's own concurrency management. But it adds complexity we don't …

If the sync logic were still embedded in the binary sensor entity (the original ~745 line version), this redesign would have touched entity lifecycle, HA platform integration, and coordinator callbacks simultaneously. Instead it was a clean swap of the orchestration engine behind a stable interface (`in_sync`, `async_start`, `async_stop`, `state_writer`). > DEVELOPER

should the coordinator own the sync manager instead of the binary sensor?

> AGENT

That's a good architectural question. Let me think through both: **Current: Binary sensor owns the manager** - Manager lifecycle tied to entity lifecycle (created in constructor, stopped on removal) - One manager per lock × slot × entity — clean 1:1 mapping - Entity provides `state_writer` callback directly - If the entity doesn't exist (e.g., coordinator is None during setup), no manager exists — silent degradation **Alternative: Coordinator owns the managers** - Coordinator already exists per-lock, created during `async_setup_internal` - Manager lifecycle tied to lock lifecycle (survives entity removal/recreation) - Managers exist even before entities are created — could start reconciling earlier - Entity registers its `state_writer` with an already-running manager - When entities are recreated (config update), the manager keeps running — no state loss The coordinator ownership model is appealing because: 1. The manager already depends on the coordinator (`self._coordinator`) 2. The manager reads coordinator data (`self._coordinator.data`) 3. The manager subscribes to coordinator updates 4. The coordinator is the right "lifetime" — it outlives entities and represents the lock's data model But there's a practical concern: the coordinator is per-lock, while managers are per-lock × slot. The coordinator would need a `dict[int, SlotSyncManager]` mapping slot numbers to managers. That's straightforward but adds responsibility to the coordinator. The bigger question: **would this actually change behavior in a meaningful way?** The current model works — managers start when entities start, stop when entities stop. The coordinator-owned model would let managers survive entity recreation, which matters during config updates (slot PIN changed → entity recreated → manager keeps running vs. being destroyed and recreated). I think it's a good future direction but not urgent. The current model is correct and tested. Moving ownership would be a lifecycle refactor — not a sync logic change. Worth noting in TODO.md but not blocking …

> DEVELOPER

this is merged, but we never confirmed whether the frontend or websocket needed any changes. No, right?

> AGENT

Right — no frontend or websocket changes needed. The tick-based sync is entirely internal to `SlotSyncManager`. The interface between the sync manager and the rest of the system didn't change: - **Entity → Manager**: Entity still creates the manager, registers `state_writer`, calls `async_start`/`async_stop`. The `in_sync` property still returns `bool | None`. - **Manager → Entity**: Manager still calls `state_writer()` when `in_sync` changes. Entity still reads `_attr_is_on` from the callback. - **Manager → Coordinator**: Manager still reads `coordinator.data` and calls `coordinator.async_refresh()`. - **Websocket**: Reads entity state via `hass.states.get()` — doesn't interact with the sync manager at all. - **Frontend**: Reads entity state via the websocket subscription — completely decoupled. The only external-facing change is the 5-second latency (entity state updates up to 5s after a change vs near-instant), but that's invisible to the websocket/frontend since they just read whatever the current entity state is.

You're right. The tick delay is fine for *sync operations* (writing codes to the lock), but the **state display** (in-sync checkmark going green) shouldn't wait for the next tick. When the coordinator data changes (push update confirms the code was set), the entity should update immediately. The problem: in the old event-driven code, coordinator updates directly triggered `_async_check_and_sync` which would detect "back in sync" and write state immediately. Now, coordinator updates just set `_dirty = True`, and the entity waits up to 5 seconds for the next tick to read the new data and flip `_in_sync` to True. The fix: separate the **read** path (detecting sync state) from the **write** path (performing set/clear operations). Reads should be instant; only writes need the tick. When `_mark_dirty` fires from a coordinator update or state change, we can immediately re-evaluate `calculate_in_sync` and update the entity state if the result changes — without performing any sync operations. The tick only handles the "out of sync → perform sync" path.

Let me compile the full research report. --- ## Matter DoorLock Cluster Research Report ### 1. Matter DoorLock Credential Model (Cluster 0x0101) The Matter DoorLock cluster uses a **two-level hierarchy**: Users and Credentials. A User is a logical entity (a person) that can hold one or more Credentials (PINs, RFID tags, biometrics, etc.). **Key relationships:** - Each **User** occupies a **UserIndex** slot (1-based, up to `NumberOfTotalUsersSupported`) - Each **Credential** occupies a **CredentialIndex** slot within a given **CredentialType** (1-based, up to `NumberOfCredentialsSupportedPerUser`) - A User can hold multiple credentials of different types - A Credential is always associated with exactly one User - The `GetUserResponse` returns a `Credentials` list of `CredentialStruct` entries showing which credentials belong to that user **CredentialStruct** (the fundamental credential reference): - `CredentialType` (CredentialTypeEnum) -- the type of credential - `CredentialIndex` (uint16) -- the slot index within that type #### Enum Definitions (from Matter 1.5 DoorLock.xml) **CredentialTypeEnum:** | Value | Name | Description | |-------|------|-------------| | 0 | ProgrammingPIN | Special master/programming PIN | | 1 | PIN | Standard numeric PIN code | | 2 | RFID | RFID tag/card | | 3 | Fingerprint | Fingerprint biometric | | 4 | FingerVein | Finger vein biometric | | 5 | Face | Facial recognition | | 6 | AliroCredentialIssuerKey | Aliro NFC-based issuer key | | 7 | AliroEvictableEndpointKey | Aliro temporary key | | 8 | AliroNonEvictableEndpointKey | Aliro permanent key | **UserTypeEnum:** | Value | Name | |-------|------| | 0 | UnrestrictedUser | | 1 | YearDayScheduleUser | | 2 | WeekDayScheduleUser | | 3 | ProgrammingUser | | 4 | NonAccessUser | | 5 | ForcedUser | | 6 | DisposableUser | | 7 | …

> AGENT

Excellent research. Let me digest the key findings and start the brainstorming process. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **The fundamental tension:** Matter separates *users* from *credentials*. A user (person) can have multiple credentials (PIN + RFID + fingerprint). Z-Wave conflates them — a "code slot" IS the user IS the PIN. LCM currently models Z-Wave's flat model (`slot → PIN`). To support Matter, we need a model that can express both without over-engineering for Z-Wave. **The write-only constraint is the game-changer.** Matter locks don't return PIN values — you can check if a slot is occupied but can't read the code. This means the integration MUST be the source of truth for PIN values. The current sync model (read actual state from lock, compare to desired) breaks for Matter. Sync becomes "push what we know and verify the slot is occupied." `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Here's the landscape I see. Let me ask the first design question: The current LCM data model is `slot → {PIN, enabled, name, conditions}`. Matter introduces a richer hierarchy: `User → [Credential(type, index, data)]`. Z-Wave is flat: `slot → code`. There are three possible abstraction strategies: **A) Keep the flat model, map Matter into it.** Each LCM "slot" maps to one Matter user with one PIN credential. Users who want RFID or multi-credential can't use LCM for that. Simplest, but limits Matter's capabilities. **B) Add a user layer on top of slots.** LCM gets a `User` concept that owns one or more credentials. For Z-Wave, user = slot (1:1). For Matter, user = UserIndex with credentials underneath. More complex, but expresses both models. **C) Model credentials directly, make users implicit.** LCM manages credentials (PIN codes), and the provider handles user creation/mapping. For Z-Wave, a credential IS a slot. For Matter, the provider auto-creates a user when setting a credential. Keeps …

Good. Next question: the write-only PIN constraint in Matter changes how sync works fundamentally. Currently, sync is **bidirectional**: read the lock's actual code → compare to desired → set/clear if different. This works for Z-Wave because we can read codes back. For Matter, sync must be **write-forward**: push the desired PIN → verify the slot is occupied (but can't verify the PIN value matches). The "in sync" check becomes: "is there a credential in the expected slot?" rather than "does the credential match?" This means `calculate_in_sync` needs to work differently per provider: **Z-Wave**: `in_sync = (lock_code == expected_pin)` — full value comparison **Matter**: `in_sync = (slot_occupied == expected_occupied)` — presence-only comparison How should the provider express this difference to the sync manager? Two options: **Option 1: Provider returns a flag indicating whether codes are comparable.** The sync manager checks `provider.supports_code_readback`. If True, compare values. If False, compare presence only. **Option 2: Provider normalizes the code value.** Z-Wave returns the actual PIN. Matter returns a sentinel (e.g., the expected PIN if occupied, empty if not). The sync manager always does value comparison, but Matter "lies" by returning what it expects rather than what's actually on the lock. This is what PR #741 already does with masked codes. Which approach?

> DEVELOPER

Option 1 but it has to be at the slot level in case some slots are readable and others aren't

> AGENT

That's a good refinement — some locks might mask certain slots but not others (we already see this with Z-Wave masked PINs where `expected_codes` resolves the mask). The readability is per-slot, not per-provider. So the coordinator data model needs to express per-slot whether the code is the actual value or just a presence indicator. Currently it's `dict[int, str | None]` where the string is the actual code (or empty for cleared). The natural evolution: the coordinator returns per-slot data that includes both the code (if readable) and whether it's comparable. Rather than a boolean flag, the simplest representation is: if the code is `None` (not empty string `""`), it means "occupied but value unknown." Empty string means "slot cleared." Actual string means "readable code." So the semantics become: - `"1234"` — slot has this code, comparable - `""` — slot is empty/cleared - `None` — slot is occupied but code is unreadable Then `calculate_in_sync` becomes: - If `lock_code` is a string (including empty): compare values as today - If `lock_code` is `None`: slot is occupied, in sync if active (we expect a code there) This uses the existing type (`dict[int, str | None]`) — `None` just gets a new meaning. Does that work, or do you want a more explicit representation?

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

Right — overloading `None` and `""` with semantic meaning is exactly the kind of implicit behavior we've been cleaning up. An explicit enum makes the state self-documenting. Something like: ```python class SlotCode(StrEnum): EMPTY = "empty" # slot has no code (cleared) UNKNOWN = "unknown" # slot has a code but we can't read it ``` Then coordinator data becomes `dict[int, str | SlotCode]` where: - `"1234"` — readable code - `SlotCode.EMPTY` — slot cleared - `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` — occupied, value unreadable This replaces the current `""` for empty and eliminates the `None` ambiguity. The sync manager's `calculate_in_sync` checks: - `SlotCode.EMPTY` + active=ON → out of sync (need to set code) - `SlotCode.EMPTY` + active=OFF → in sync - `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` + active=ON → in sync (code is there, can't verify value) - `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` + active=OFF → out of sync (need to clear) - `"1234"` + active=ON → compare to expected PIN - `"1234"` + active=OFF → out of sync (need to clear) Does this capture the states correctly, or is there a case I'm missing?

If it can resolve, it returns the expected PIN (the coordinator then reports the PIN as if it were readable). If it can't resolve, it returns `None` and the slot gets skipped. With `SlotCode.UNKNOWN`, masked PINs could just be reported as `UNKNOWN` — "there's a code here but I can't read it." The sync logic then treats it the same as Matter: if we expect a code there, it's in sync. No need for the `_resolve_pin_if_masked` guessing game. This would: - **Remove `_resolve_pin_if_masked` entirely** — the most complex method in `zwave_js.py` - **Remove `coordinator.expected_codes`** — no longer needed for masked PIN resolution - **Unify the code path** — masked Z-Wave, Matter, and any future write-only provider all use the same `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` → "in sync if expected" logic - **Eliminate the startup race** — `expected_codes` was added specifically because `_resolve_pin_if_masked` read entity state during startup. With `UNKNOWN`, there's nothing to resolve. The only downside: for masked Z-Wave locks, the code sensor entity would show "Unknown" instead of the expected PIN. Currently it shows the resolved PIN (which is a guess based on what we configured, not what the lock actually has). Showing "Unknown" is arguably more honest. Want to include this simplification in the design?

The sync logic uses `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` natively, and the masked PIN resolution is a Z-Wave-specific optimization layered on top. No breaking change, no new behavior for users. So the flow for Z-Wave masked PINs becomes: 1. Cache returns `****` → Z-Wave provider detects mask → returns `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` 2. `_resolve_pin_if_masked` sees `UNKNOWN` → checks `expected_codes` → if match, upgrades to the expected PIN string 3. Coordinator data shows the resolved PIN (same as today) 4. If can't resolve → stays `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` → sync treats as "occupied, in sync if expected" This is cleaner than today because: - The mask detection (`"*" * len(value)`) stays in `_resolve_pin_if_masked` but the input is already typed as `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` rather than a raw asterisk string - `_resolve_pin_if_masked` becomes simpler — no more "is this all asterisks?" check, just "is this UNKNOWN?" ## SlotCode Enum Data Model Design ### Problem The coordinator stores `dict[int, str | None]` where empty string means "cleared" and None is ambiguous. Matter locks can't return PIN values — they only report whether a slot is occupied. This implicit model can't express that distinction. ### The enum ```python class SlotCode(StrEnum): EMPTY = "empty" # slot cleared UNKNOWN = "unknown" # occupied but value unreadable ``` Coordinator data becomes `dict[int, str | SlotCode]`: - `"1234"` — readable code (Z-Wave unmasked) - `SlotCode.EMPTY` — slot cleared (any provider) - `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` — occupied, can't read value (Matter, Z-Wave masked unresolved) ### Sync behavior | Lock code | Active | In sync? | Why | |---|---|---|---| | `"1234"` | ON | `pin == "1234"` | Compare values | | `"1234"` | OFF | No | Need to clear | | `EMPTY` | ON | No | Need to set | | `EMPTY` | OFF | Yes | Correctly empty | | `UNKNOWN` | ON | Yes | Code is there, can't verify value | | `UNKNOWN` | OFF | No | Need to clear | ### Z-Wave masked PINs (backwards compatible) Today `_resolve_pin_if_masked` detects asterisk patterns and guesses the value from `expected_codes`. With this change: 1. Provider detects mask → returns `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` 2. `_resolve_pin_if_masked` sees `UNKNOWN` + has expected PIN → upgrades to the PIN string 3. Coordinator shows the resolved PIN (same as today for users) 4. If can't resolve → stays `UNKNOWN` → sync treats as "in sync if we expect a code" The method gets simpler — no more asterisk pattern matching, just check for `UNKNOWN`. ### What stays the same - `BaseLock` interface (`set_usercode`, `clear_usercode`, `get_usercodes`) — unchanged - Config entry data format — unchanged - Frontend behavior for Z-Wave users — unchanged - `expected_codes` — stays for …

Three call sites: 1. **Line 255** — inside `_handle_usercode_value_update` (push update handler). Resolves before pushing to coordinator. 2. **Line 492** — inside `async_set_usercode`. Resolves to compare "is the code already set to this value?" before sending a set command. 3. **Line 624** — inside `async_get_usercodes`. Resolves when building the full slot map from cache. All three resolve eagerly — converting `UNKNOWN` to the expected PIN as early as possible. The coordinator then stores the resolved PIN, and everything downstream sees a "readable" code. With `SlotCode.UNKNOWN`, we could defer resolution: the coordinator stores `UNKNOWN`, and resolution only happens at the point where an actual value comparison is needed. That's: - **Sync's `calculate_in_sync`** — but with the new logic, `UNKNOWN` + active=ON = in sync. No resolution needed. - **The code sensor display** — could show "Unknown" or resolve for display only. - **The `async_set_usercode` duplicate check** (line 492) — needs to know if the current code matches to skip redundant sets. This is the ONE place where resolution actually matters. So the resolution would move from 3 call sites to 1 — the duplicate check in `async_set_usercode`. The coordinator stores `UNKNOWN` honestly, sync handles it natively, and resolution is only done when we need a value comparison for deduplication. Want me to update the design to reflect this — resolution deferred to `async_set_usercode` only?

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` The current system completely flattens slot status into a binary: `in_use` (True/False). Z-Wave actually has three states (Available/Enabled/Disabled), and Matter has its own (Available/OccupiedEnabled/OccupiedDisabled). The `DISABLED` state is interesting — a slot can have a code but be firmware-disabled, which is invisible to LCM today. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Good context. Here's my first question: **What problem are we actually trying to solve with SlotStatus?** The TODO lists it but the "why" isn't crisp. I see a few possible motivations: - **A)** Prevent sync conflicts — if the lock firmware has disabled a slot, LCM shouldn't keep trying to set a code on it (or should explicitly re-enable it first) - **B)** Surface lock-side status to the user — show in the UI whether a slot is firmware-enabled/disabled, so the user understands why a code might not work - **C)** Enable firmware-level disable as a feature — let users disable a slot at the lock level instead of just clearing the code (preserves the code but deactivates it) - **D)** Matter prep — Matter's `OccupiedDisabled` state needs representation and we need to handle it correctly in sync Which of these are you trying to address? (Could be multiple.)

Here is my analysis. --- ## Type: `SlotCode(StrEnum)` ### Invariants Identified 1. **Two and only two sentinel states exist**: EMPTY (cleared) and UNKNOWN (occupied but unreadable) 2. **Sentinel values are distinct from actual PIN codes**: The string values "empty" and "unknown" serve as sentinel markers that should never collide with real PINs 3. **SlotCode values are immutable enum members**: Cannot be extended or modified at runtime 4. **Coordinator data maps slots to exactly one of three categories**: a readable string PIN, SlotCode.EMPTY, or SlotCode.UNKNOWN ### Ratings - **Encapsulation**: 5/10 `StrEnum` inherits from `str`, which means `SlotCode.EMPTY == "empty"` is `True`. This is a **leaky abstraction** -- the sentinel's string value is exposed as its identity. Any code doing `==` comparisons (rather than `is` identity checks) will conflate the sentinel with its backing string. The PR consistently uses `is` checks, which is correct, but this is an invariant enforced only by developer discipline, not by the type system. - **Invariant Expression**: 6/10 The union type `str | SlotCode` is clear about intent, and the docstring explains the semantics well. However, the type annotation itself does not prevent confusion: since `SlotCode` IS a `str` (via `StrEnum`), the union is technically redundant at runtime -- every `SlotCode` value already satisfies `str`. A type checker can distinguish them, but the type literally says "string or string-subclass," which is semantically weak. The real invariant is "either an opaque PIN string that is NOT a sentinel, or a sentinel enum member," and the type system cannot express this. - **Invariant Usefulness**: 8/10 This is a strong improvement over the previous `str | None` model. Replacing the overloaded empty string and None with explicit sentinels removes …

Let me check what Matter lock code support looks like in HA core.

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` Key Matter findings relevant to SlotStatus: - **PINs are write-only** — `GetCredentialStatus` only returns `credential_exists` (boolean) + user binding, never the actual PIN - **Users have tri-state status**: `available`, `occupied_enabled`, `occupied_disabled` - **Credentials only have binary status**: occupied or not (no enabled/disabled at credential level) - **Users must exist before credentials** — it's a two-level hierarchy `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Now back to the question you reframed: **given Matter's write-only PINs and what we already have with `SlotCode`, do we actually need `SlotStatus`?** Let me think about what `SlotCode` already covers vs what `SlotStatus` would add: **What `SlotCode` handles today:** - `EMPTY` — slot has no code (Z-Wave `AVAILABLE`, Matter credential not occupied) - `UNKNOWN` — slot has a code but we can't read it (Z-Wave masked PINs, Matter all PINs) - `str` — slot has a readable code **What `SlotStatus` would add:** - Whether a slot is firmware-**disabled** (Z-Wave `CodeSlotStatus.DISABLED`, Matter `occupied_disabled`) **The practical question:** Does the firmware-disabled state matter for LCM's sync logic? Here's my take: **No, not for reliable sync.** Here's why: 1. **Z-Wave `DISABLED` is basically unused** — almost no Z-Wave locks support it. The Z-Wave provider already ignores it entirely and things work fine. 2. **Matter `occupied_disabled` is at the USER level, not the credential level.** LCM would need to manage users AND credentials. When LCM sets a credential, it controls the user status too (the `SetCredential` command can set user_status in the same call). So LCM would always create users as `occupied_enabled`. 3. **If a user manually disables a slot at the lock keypad**, LCM's next sync would just re-enable it (since LCM's desired state is "active with this PIN"). That's the correct behavior — LCM is the source of truth. 4. **The only scenario where SlotStatus matters** is if LCM wants to *detect* …

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` This commit demonstrates a clean separation of concerns: the **backend** now passes domain state as-is (`"empty"`, `"unknown"`, actual PIN, `null`), and the **frontend** decides how to display each. This is better than the previous approach where the backend normalized everything to `null` — that lost information and required a separate `has_code` boolean to recover it. Passing raw state through keeps the API contract simple and the presentation layer independent. The push handler fix is also significant: `slot_in_use is not False` (instead of `code_slot_in_use`) means we correctly treat masked PINs as `UNKNOWN` even when the cache status is unknown (`None`), which prevents raw `"****"` strings from leaking into coordinator data. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Pushed. All 364 Python tests and 326 TS tests pass. The changes: - **Backend**: Simplified websocket serialization — SlotCode values pass through as strings, removed `has_code` - **Frontend**: Updated `_hasCode`, `_getCodeClass`, `_formatCode`, `_formatLockCode`, and edit prefill to handle `"empty"` and `"unknown"` sentinel strings - **Push handler**: Fixed all-zeros to check `slot_in_use is False`, and masked codes now resolve to `UNKNOWN` when `slot_in_use` is `None` - **Tests**: Updated Python and TS tests to match the new serialization contract

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` The audit found the codebase is well-protected at the websocket/frontend layer — `_serialize_slot` masks codes by default and only reveals them when `reveal=True`. Logging, notifications, events, and service responses all avoid exposing PINs. The one notable finding is the **code sensor entity** (`sensor.py:native_value`) which exposes the PIN as its entity state. However, this is **intentional by design** — the sensor IS the code state entity, and its purpose is to hold the current code value. It's marked as `EntityCategory.DIAGNOSTIC` and the websocket layer masks it before sending to the frontend. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Here's what I found: ## PIN Exposure Audit Results ### Properly Masked (all good) - **Websocket responses** — `_serialize_slot` and `_build_lock_status` mask codes by default, only reveal with explicit `reveal=True` - **Logging** — no PINs logged in plaintext anywhere (uses `"****"` for SlotCode check in debug logs) - **Notifications** — reference slot numbers, never actual PINs - **Events** — only slot name/number, no PIN data - **Service responses** — no PIN data returned - **Text entity** — uses `TextMode.PASSWORD` for UI masking ### Code Sensor Entity State The agent flagged `sensor.py:native_value` returning the actual PIN. This is intentional — the sensor's entire purpose is to hold the code value so the sync manager can compare desired vs actual state. The websocket layer sits in front and masks it before it reaches the frontend. ### Unused Masking Helper There's a `mask_pin()` function in `util.py` (creates deterministic hash like `pin#a1b2c3d4`) that isn't currently used anywhere. Could be useful for debug logging if we ever need to log code-related operations without exposing the actual value. **Bottom line: the masking layer is solid.** No PINs leak to the frontend, logs, notifications, or events. The sensor state exposure is by design and properly gated by the websocket masking.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` `expected_codes` is a computed property that reads config entry data on every access to build a `{slot: pin}` dict. It's doing two things: (1) providing the configured PIN for display resolution in the sensor, and (2) answering "does LCM expect a code here?" for Z-Wave event filtering. Both consumers only need a subset of what it computes — the sensor needs the PIN for one slot, and the Z-Wave provider only needs a boolean. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` The simplification options: **Option A: Inline the config lookup at each consumer.** Remove `expected_codes` property, have sensor and `_slot_expects_pin` read from config entry directly. The logic is simple — `get_entry_data(entry, CONF_SLOTS, {})[slot].get(CONF_PIN, "")`. **Option B: Split into two focused methods.** `get_expected_pin(slot) -> str | None` for the sensor and `slot_expects_pin(slot) -> bool` on the coordinator. Remove the dict-building property. **Option C: Move `_slot_expects_pin` to use config entry directly** (it already has access via `self.coordinator._config_entry`), and have the sensor resolve UNKNOWN inline too. Remove `expected_codes` entirely. Both consumers have access to the config entry — the sensor via `self.config_entry` (from the entity base class) and `_slot_expects_pin` via `self.coordinator`. So they don't need the coordinator to mediate. This is a design choice that shapes how config data flows through the system. What's your preference — should the coordinator keep mediating config lookups, or should consumers go directly to the config entry?

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

Option B. The coordinator is the right place to mediate config lookups — it already owns the config entry reference and the consumers shouldn't need to know the internal config structure (`CONF_SLOTS`, `CONF_ENABLED`, `CONF_PIN`). But the current `expected_codes` builds a full dict every access when nobody needs the full dict. Two focused methods are cleaner: ```python def get_expected_pin(self, slot_num: int) -> str | None: """Return configured PIN for a slot, or None if disabled/unconfigured.""" def slot_expects_pin(self, slot_num: int) -> bool: """Return whether LCM expects a PIN on this slot.""" ``` `slot_expects_pin` is just `bool(self.get_expected_pin(slot_num))`, so it's really one method plus a convenience wrapper. This also moves `_slot_expects_pin` out of the Z-Wave provider (where it doesn't belong — it's not Z-Wave-specific) and into the coordinator. Want me to go ahead?

Now, based on the exploration, here's what I see: `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Unload asymmetry**: Setup goes through `async_update_listener()` which orchestrates slot/lock add/remove via callbacks. Unload bypasses this entirely — it just calls `async_unload_platforms()` directly, meaning lock-removed callbacks never fire during entry unload. The fix is to route unload through the same update listener path by treating it as "remove all slots and locks." **Catch-all state tracking**: When the sync manager starts before its entities exist in the registry, it subscribes to ALL HA state changes and filters in the callback. Once in catch-all mode, it never upgrades to targeted. The comment says "modifying subscriptions from within a callback causes timing issues." The fix is to not do it from within a callback — instead, check on the next tick and upgrade there. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` These are two independent changes. Let me ask about each: **1. Symmetric unload** — The simplest approach: in `async_unload_entry()`, before unloading platforms, call `async_update_listener()` with an empty config (no slots, no locks). This triggers the normal removal flow — lock removed callbacks fire, entities get proper cleanup, then platforms unload cleanly. Does that match what you had in mind? **2. Catch-all → targeted upgrade** — The timing issue was about modifying subscriptions from within a state change callback. But we can safely upgrade at the start of `_async_tick_impl()` — that runs on a timer, not inside a callback. If the manager is in catch-all mode and `_build_entity_id_map()` succeeds, we unsub the catch-all, sub to targeted, and continue. Should I go with that approach?

If omitted, finds first empty slot - `user_name` (optional, str): Display name for the user - `user_type` (optional, str): Type of user (defaults to "unrestricted_user") - Options: `unrestricted_user`, `year_day_schedule_user`, `week_day_schedule_user`, `programming_user`, `non_access_user`, `forced_user`, `disposable_user`, `expiring_user`, `schedule_restricted_user`, `remote_only_user` - `credential_rule` (optional, str): Credential combination rule (defaults to "single") - Options: `single`, `dual`, `tri` **Return value:** ```python { "user_index": 1 # The slot number where user was created/modified } ``` **Behavior:** - **Create new user** …

Let me present the design concisely — we've already resolved the key decisions. ## Matter Provider Design ### Architecture `MatterLock` extends `BaseLock`. It uses **HA services** (`matter.set_lock_credential`, `matter.clear_lock_credential`, etc.) for all CRUD operations. PINs are write-only — `async_get_usercodes` returns `SlotCode.UNKNOWN` for occupied slots, `SlotCode.EMPTY` for cleared ones. ### Slot↔Credential Mapping LCM's concept of "slot number" maps to Matter's credential index. Each credential needs a user — when setting a code, we use `set_lock_credential` without `user_index` to let the device auto-create the user. We track `{slot_num: user_index}` to know which user to clear when clearing a slot. ### Methods | Method | Implementation | |--------|---------------| | `async_get_usercodes()` | Call `get_lock_users` → for each user with PIN credentials, call `get_lock_credential_status` per credential index → return `UNKNOWN`/`EMPTY` | | `async_set_usercode()` | Call `set_lock_credential(pin, credential_index=slot_num)` — auto-creates user if needed | | `async_clear_usercode()` | Call `clear_lock_credential(pin, credential_index=slot_num)`, then `clear_lock_user(user_index)` if user has no remaining credentials | | `async_is_integration_connected()` | Check Matter integration is loaded | | `async_is_device_available()` | Check node availability via `get_lock_info` | ### Push Updates Matter doesn't push credential changes, but we get `LockOperation` events. For now, rely on polling (`usercode_scan_interval = 5 minutes`). Push subscription can be added later. ### Test Architecture Mirror HA's Matter test pattern: - Mock `hass.services.async_call` for Matter service calls (not raw Matter client) - Sequential `side_effect` for multi-step operations - No fixture JSON needed (we're calling HA services, not raw Matter commands) Does this design look right?

For Matter locks, every slot gets set (all are write-only). **For the wiki/docs**, the key points to document: - Locks with masked PINs (Z-Wave) or write-only PINs (Matter) will always re-set codes on HA restart - This is by design — it ensures the lock always has the correct PIN - PIN changes are detected even when the lock code can't be read back - The extra set operation is safe — the lock handles idempotent writes

two follow up questions: 1. are there any other places where concurrency makes order of operations fragile? 2. I forget the second

> AGENT

For #1, let me think through the concurrent paths in the sync system:

> AGENT

The main concurrent paths are: 1. **Tick timer** (every 5s) → `_async_tick` → `_async_tick_impl` → `_perform_sync` 2. **State change callback** → `_mark_dirty` (synchronous, just sets flag) 3. **Coordinator update callback** → `_mark_dirty` (synchronous, just sets flag) 4. **Push updates from provider** → `coordinator.push_update` → triggers coordinator listeners → `_mark_dirty` The tick-based design is inherently safe here — `_mark_dirty` just sets a boolean flag, and `_async_tick` is the only thing that acts on it. Since HA's event loop is single-threaded, there's no true parallelism. The tick reads `_dirty`, clears it, then does work. If a state change fires during the work, `_dirty` gets set again and the next tick handles it. The one fragile area I see: **`_perform_sync` sets `_last_set_pin` then the provider fires an optimistic `push_update` with `SlotCode.UNKNOWN`.** The order matters: if `push_update` triggers `_mark_dirty` before `_last_set_pin` is recorded, the next tick would see UNKNOWN with stale `_last_set_pin`. But since this is all in the same `await` chain (set → record → push happens inside the provider's `async_internal_set_usercode`), and the event loop doesn't yield between `_last_set_pin = ...` and the push... let me verify:

> AGENT

Here's the issue: `async_internal_set_usercode` is awaited, and inside it the provider may call `coordinator.push_update()` which fires `_mark_dirty`. That happens *before* line 290 sets `_last_set_pin`. So the sequence is: 1. `await async_internal_set_usercode(...)` — fires push_update inside 2. push_update → coordinator listener → `_mark_dirty()` — sets `_dirty = True` 3. Back in `_perform_sync`: `_last_set_pin = pin_state` — recorded AFTER dirty was set This is actually fine because `_dirty` just means "check again on next tick." By the time the next tick runs, `_last_set_pin` is already set. But to be safe, let me move `_last_set_pin` before the await:

> AGENT

Wait — but if the set *fails*, we've now recorded a PIN we didn't actually set. Let me check error handling:

> AGENT

Right — if the set fails, `_last_set_pin` is wrong. The safest approach: keep it after the await, since the ordering issue I identified isn't actually a problem (next tick is a separate event loop iteration, `_last_set_pin` is set before then):

> AGENT

So to answer your question properly: **The concurrency model is actually solid.** The tick-based design avoids most ordering issues: - `_mark_dirty` is synchronous (just sets a bool) — no race conditions - `_async_tick` is the sole actor — reads dirty, clears it, does work - State changes during a tick set dirty again → handled on next tick - HA's event loop is single-threaded — no true parallelism **The one area that's theoretically fragile** is the push_update path inside `async_internal_set_usercode`: the provider's optimistic push triggers `_mark_dirty` before `_last_set_pin` is recorded. But this is harmless — `_dirty` just means "re-evaluate on next tick," and by the time the next tick runs (5s later or next event loop iteration), `_last_set_pin` is already set. **No actual concurrency bugs found.** The tick-based architecture is specifically designed to eliminate these — it's why we moved from event-driven sync to tick-based in the first place.

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` The tick-based architecture pays dividends here. Most concurrency concerns in event-driven systems (callback ordering, race conditions between subscribers) are eliminated because the sync manager's only actor is the periodic tick. State changes just set a flag; the tick reads it and acts. HA's single-threaded event loop handles the rest. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` **Summary: the codebase is solid.** Here's the audit: | Area | Risk | Status | |------|------|--------| | Platform setup ordering | Low | Sequential `async_forward_entry_setups` | | Slot removal in `asyncio.gather` | Low | Independent slots, snapshot iteration | | Sync manager entity discovery | Medium | Mitigated — retry every 5s tick, now upgrades to targeted | | Coordinator vs entity timing | Low | Serialized in `_async_setup_new_locks` | | Config update during setup | Low | Update listener registered before platforms, proper sequencing | | Push subscription timing | Low | Deferred with retry mechanism | | Callback registry iteration | Low | Registration completes before invocation; removal uses snapshots | | Lock removal ordering | Low | Sync callbacks fire before async teardown | **One minor hardening suggestion:** The adder callback loops (lines 192, 205, 220 in `callbacks.py`) iterate dicts directly rather than snapshots. This is safe today because registration is complete before invocation, but `list(...)` snapshots would be defensive. Not blocking — just cosmetic. No action items. The architecture is well-designed for HA's concurrency model.

> DEVELOPER

if we can't address 14, should we really be setting the PIN on restart every time?

> AGENT

Good point. If we can't detect external changes (#14), then setting on restart doesn't actually protect against that scenario either — it only protects against "config changed while HA was down." And that's a narrow case. The trade-off is: **Setting on every restart:** - Pro: Guarantees correctness if config changed while HA was down - Con: Extra write to the lock on every restart for every masked slot (battery impact for Z-Wave, unnecessary network traffic for Matter) - Con: Doesn't actually fix the "someone changed code at keypad" problem anyway **Not setting on restart (persist `_last_set_pin`):** - Pro: No unnecessary writes on restart - Pro: Same behavior as unmasked locks (only set when out of sync) - Con: If user changed PIN in config while HA was down, won't detect until next PIN change triggers a diff - But: How realistic is "config changed while HA was down"? Config changes go through the UI which requires HA to be running The "config changed while HA was down" scenario basically can't happen — you can't change config without HA running. The only way would be someone editing `.storage` files directly, which is not a supported workflow. I think we should **persist `_last_set_pin`** and avoid the restart writes. We could store it in the coordinator data or config entry runtime data. What do you think — persist it, or accept the restart writes as a known conservative behavior?

> DEVELOPER

I agree. And in the future we can offer an option to always write (on restart, on poll, not sure how aggressive we'd be but its an option)

Right — the restart set is effectively free drift detection for masked/write-only slots. And it aligns with the drift detection design we discussed: for UNKNOWN slots, "detect drift" = "always re-set" since you can't compare. Let's keep the current behavior (set on restart) and move on. The PR is ready.

This is inconsistent: line 131 defensively uses `.get("credential_type")`, acknowledging the field might be absent, but line 132 assumes `credential_index` is always present. Both fields come from the same untrusted service response. **Hidden Errors:** A `KeyError` would propagate up through the coordinator as an unexpected exception rather than a `LockCodeManagerError`, which could cause different retry/handling behavior than the coordinator expects. **User Impact:** Users see a raw Python traceback in logs instead of a clear "malformed response from Matter lock" message. The coordinator may not handle it correctly since it expects `LockCodeManagerError` subclasses from providers (as documented in the base class docstring). **Recommendation:** Either use `.get()` with a guard, or wrap the iteration in a try/except that converts `KeyError`/`TypeError`/`ValueError` to `LockCodeManagerError` with context about which user/credential was malformed. ```python # Option A: defensive .get() with guard for credential in user.get("credentials", []): if …

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` HA's repairs flow is a multi-step data_entry_flow — it can show menus, forms, and execute actions. In theory, a repair could import a blueprint and create an `input_number` helper. But that's complex: importing a blueprint requires calling `blueprint.import_blueprint` service, and creating a helper requires the `input_number.create` websocket command. Both are doable from a RepairsFlow, but it's a lot of plumbing for a deprecation migration. The simpler approach: persistent notification with a link to the wiki Blueprints page. One notification regardless of how many entries are affected. The migration strips `CONF_NUMBER_OF_USES` from all entries. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` The repair flow with auto-import is cool but complex — it would need to: 1. Call `blueprint.import_blueprint` to import the YAML 2. Create an `input_number` helper via `input_number.create` 3. Create an automation from the blueprint linking the helper to each slot 4. Handle errors at each step That's a lot of moving parts for a migration. My recommendation: **persistent notification** for now, with a clear link to the wiki. We can always add a repair flow later if users struggle with the migration. Want me to go with persistent notification, or do you want the repair flow?

Pushed. Two additions: 1. **Notification target** — optional `notify_target` input (text selector). When uses hit 0 and the slot is disabled, calls the notification service with "Code slot has been disabled — usage limit reached." Leave empty to skip. 2. **Unlimited uses (-1)** — when counter is -1, the PIN used trigger exits early with `stop:`. Counter stays at -1, slot never disables. The re-enable reset also supports -1 (`initial_uses: -1` resets to unlimited on re-enable). The condition for re-enable reset changed from `> 0` to `!= 0` to handle both positive and -1.

are there any custom jinja templates we should offer as well?

> AGENT

Good question. Looking at what LCM exposes and common automation needs: **Useful ones we could offer:** 1. **`lock_code_manager_entities(config_entry_id, slot_num)`** — resolves the entity IDs for a slot (pin, name, enabled, active, event) without users having to regex-match `integration_entities()`. This is the pattern that broke the blueprint initially. 2. **`lock_code_manager_slot_name(config_entry_id, slot_num)`** — shorthand to get the slot name text entity value. Useful in notifications: "Guest (slot 3) used the PIN" instead of just "slot 3". But these would need to be registered as custom Jinja globals/filters in HA, which requires `template.environment.globals` patching — a bit hacky and could break on HA updates. The more practical approach: **no custom Jinja**. The blueprints already abstract away the entity resolution. For users writing their own automations, the existing HA template functions (`integration_entities`, `config_entry_attr`, `state_attr`) are sufficient — the debugging section in the blueprint descriptions shows how to use them. Adding custom Jinja is complexity we'd need to maintain and it couples us to HA's template internals. I'd skip it unless users ask for it. What do you think?

> DEVELOPER

HA allows you to define custom Jinja. We could offer them the same way we offer the blueprints I believe, although I am not sure how to "import"

> AGENT

So HA loads `.jinja` files from `config/custom_templates/` at startup. They're used with `{% from 'filename.jinja' import macro_name %}` in templates. But there's no "import" mechanism like blueprints — users manually copy the files. Let me check how they're used:

> AGENT

So the pattern is: put `.jinja` files in `config/custom_templates/`, then use `{% from 'filename.jinja' import macro_name %}` in any template. Since there's no import button like blueprints, users would need to manually copy the file. We could: 1. Ship a `.jinja` file in the repo and document how to copy it 2. Have LCM automatically copy it to `custom_templates/` during setup Option 2 is cleaner — LCM can install its Jinja macros on setup, similar to how it installs the Lovelace resources. But that's a feature addition, not a quick win. For now, this is a "nice to have later" item. The blueprints handle the common cases, and power users can use `integration_entities()` + regex directly. Want me to add it as a TODO item, or do you want to pursue it now?

> AGENT

Both are handled by `CODE_SLOT_SCHEMA` which still accepts `number_of_uses` for backward compatibility. The schema validates incoming data but doesn't know if it's a new slot or existing. For both cases, the fix is the same pattern as the websocket: check if the slot already had `number_of_uses` before accepting it. **For options flow (#2):** In `async_update_listener`, when we compare old vs new slot configs, if new config has `number_of_uses` but old config doesn't, strip it. **For config flow (#1):** New entries have no "before" — so any `number_of_uses` on a brand new entry should be stripped. The cleanest single place: strip `number_of_uses` from new slots in `async_update_listener` before processing. This handles both cases since: - New entries go through `async_update_listener` (data → options → listener) - Options updates go through `async_update_listener`

This means: - Every 5-minute poll re-scans for untagged codes and attempts to tag them, even if tagging already failed for legitimate reasons (e.g., a code the user intentionally left untagged). - The add/delete calls bypass `_execute_rate_limited` because they go through the internal helpers directly rather than through `async_internal_set_usercode`/`async_internal_clear_usercode`, so there is no rate limiting or duplicate checking on these operations. - If the add succeeds but the delete fails and the rollback also fails, the next poll will attempt the same tagging again on the (now-duplicate) untagged code, potentially creating more duplicates each cycle. **Suggested fix:** Extract the auto-tagging into a separate method (e.g., `_async_tag_unmanaged_codes`) that runs once during `async_setup()`, or at most during `async_hard_refresh_codes()`. The regular `async_get_usercodes()` poll should only read and classify codes that are already tagged, treating untagged codes as not belonging to Lock Code Manager. ### 2. `_async_delete_code` deletes by name, …

The current code calls `_async_add_code(tagged_name, usercode)` first, then conditionally deletes the old code. When updating a PIN on an existing slot where the name does not change (i.e., `existing_full_name == tagged_name`), the `add_code` call will hit the duplicate name validation and raise `ServiceValidationError`, which gets wrapped as `LockDisconnected`. This means every PIN-only update on an existing Schlage code will fail at runtime. The test `test_set_usercode_preserves_existing_name` does not catch this because it mocks the service handler, bypassing the real validation logic. **Suggested fix:** When the tagged name matches the existing full name, delete-then-add (accepting the brief window of data loss for same-name updates).
